I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um... well, I've been having some trouble at work lately. I manage a clinic. \n\nIt's just... I'll sit down to do an EPIC quality report, or I'll be in a metrics meeting, and even though I fully intend to pay attention, my mind just drifts. Within a few minutes, I'm rereading the same numbers over and over, or I realize I completely missed the point of the discussion. \n\nIt's been happening a lot, so... I guess that's what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Besides having trouble focusing, do you find yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or like you have excess energy that's hard to control?\n\nPatient: Um, no. Not really. I don't think I'm restless... or have excess energy. It's just the trouble paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks or months, have you been feeling down, sad, or depressed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really felt sad. Just... frustrated with work, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this trouble with concentration? Has this been going on for years, or is this something relatively new for you?\n\nPatient: Um... it's been a while, I suppose. About two years and four months, maybe? \n\nThat's... that's when I got promoted. The new role came with a much larger portfolio, and... I guess the ways I used to keep track of things just stopped working. It just became too much.\n\nDoctor: Before you got promoted two years ago, did you ever have similar trouble concentrating or staying focused on tasks?\n\nPatient: Um... yes, I suppose. My mother actually had to sit with me while I finished my homework... just because I would lose track of assignments, or forget my books at school. I don't really do that. I'm not really one to interrupt or rush into things. It's more just... the focusing part, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did teachers or family members ever comment on you being hyperactive, restless, or having trouble sitting still in class or at home?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think so. I was pretty quiet, mostly. I don't remember anyone ever saying anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Besides work and school, have your attention problems caused you trouble in other areas of your life—like managing your personal finances, keeping up with household tasks, or maintaining relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it's mostly work, I suppose. I mean... my delays with the clinic reporting deadlines are starting to affect things. Other teams end up waiting on me. And... I manage those things fine. I just... handle them when I need to. I haven't noticed any major issues there.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that really interests you or that you find engaging—like a hobby or something you enjoy—do you have the same trouble concentrating, or are you able to focus pretty well on those things?\n\nPatient: Um... I think I'm okay with things I enjoy. I mean, if I really like doing it, I can usually focus fine. It's just... the other stuff. But hobbies are usually okay.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were around 6 to 12 years old—before you got to high school—do you remember your parents or teachers being concerned about your grades or schoolwork because of the attention or careless mistakes, even if nobody called it 'ADHD' or used that term?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I suppose so. Even back in elementary school, I remember teachers writing that I was bright, but... careless. And my mother... she usually had to sit with me while I finished my homework. I just had a habit of losing track of assignments, or... forgetting my books at school. So, yes.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work doing routine administrative tasks that aren't especially interesting to you—like responding to emails or filing paperwork—do you have trouble focusing on those, or is it mainly the bigger, more complex projects like the EPIC reports that cause the problem?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I guess the routine stuff gets done eventually. It's... mostly the bigger things, I suppose.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Just... frustrated with work, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this trouble with concentration? Has this been going on for years, or is this something relatively new for you?\n\nPatient: Um... it's been a while, I suppose. About two years and four months, maybe? \n\nThat's... that's when I got promoted. The new role came with a much larger portfolio, and... I guess the ways I used to keep track of things just stopped working. It just became too much.\n\nDoctor: Before you got promoted two years ago, did you ever have similar trouble concentrating or staying focused on tasks?\n\nPatient: Um... yes, I suppose. Even back in elementary school... I remember teachers writing that I was bright but careless. My mother actually had to sit with me while I finished my homework... just because I would lose track of assignments, or forget my books at school. So... yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things without thinking them through first, or do you have trouble waiting your turn or interrupting others?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't really do that. I'm not really one to interrupt or rush into things. It's more just... the focusing part, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did teachers or family members ever comment on you being hyperactive, restless, or having trouble sitting still in class or at home?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think so. I was pretty quiet, mostly. I don't remember anyone ever saying anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Besides work and school, have your attention problems caused you trouble in other areas of your life—like managing your personal finances, keeping up with household tasks, or maintaining relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it's mostly work, I suppose. I mean... my delays with the clinic reporting deadlines are starting to affect things. Other teams end up waiting on me. And... I guess outside of work, I used to help lead these volunteer health workshops, but... I had to step back from some of the leadership parts. I just couldn't keep all the planning pieces moving the way I needed to. So... yes, it does affect things.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you ever have trouble waiting in line, or did you feel like you needed to be moving around or doing something active most of the time, even if people didn't specifically comment on it?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't remember feeling like that. I was fine just sitting. I didn't really need to be moving around.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned work and volunteering have been affected. Setting those aside for a moment—have your attention problems caused you trouble managing things like paying bills on time, remembering appointments, or keeping track of personal responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I manage those things fine. I just... handle them when I need to. I haven't noticed any major issues there.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that really interests you or that you find engaging—like a hobby or something you enjoy—do you have the same trouble concentrating, or are you able to focus pretty well on those things?\n\nPatient: Um... I think I'm okay with things I enjoy. I mean, if I really like doing it, I can usually focus fine. It's just... the other stuff. But hobbies are usually okay.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were around 6 to 12 years old—before you got to high school—do you remember your parents or teachers being concerned about your grades or schoolwork because of the attention or careless mistakes, even if nobody called it 'ADHD' or used that term?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I suppose so. Even back in elementary school, I remember teachers writing that I was bright, but... careless. And my mother... she usually had to sit with me while I finished my homework. I just had a habit of losing track of assignments, or... forgetting my books at school. So, yes.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work doing routine administrative tasks that aren't especially interesting to you—like responding to emails or filing paperwork—do you have trouble focusing on those, or is it mainly the bigger, more complex projects like the EPIC reports that cause the problem?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I guess the routine stuff gets done eventually. It's... mostly the bigger things, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: When you're reading something you find genuinely interesting—like a novel, article, or news story you actually want to read—can you sit down and read for a while without your mind drifting, or do you still find your attention wandering even with things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I suppose... if I really want to read it, I can usually get through it. I don't really notice my mind wandering too much if it's something I actually like.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a meeting or discussion that you find genuinely interesting or important—something that really matters to you—do you have an easier time paying attention and staying focused, or do you still find your mind drifting away even then?\n\nPatient: Um... if it's important, I think I do okay. I mean, I try to pay attention. I guess I don't really notice drifting off if it's something I care about.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something at work that's both important AND interesting to you—something you actually care about and want to do well on—do you still find your attention drifting, or are you able to stay focused on those things?\n\nPatient: I guess... if it's something I really want to do, I can usually focus on it. I don't think my mind drifts too much then. I just do the work.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that routine administrative tasks eventually get done. When you do complete those less interesting tasks—like responding to emails or filing—do you find that it just takes you longer than it should, or do you sometimes make careless mistakes even though you're trying to be careful?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean, they get done eventually. I haven't really kept track of how long it takes or... if I make mistakes. I just try to get through them.\n\nDoctor: When you do finally complete those routine tasks like emails or filing—the ones that aren't very interesting—do you notice that you're making careless mistakes in them, even though you're trying to be accurate?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I mean, maybe a typo here or there, but nothing major. I just send them and move on. I don't really go back and check.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school—elementary through high school—did you struggle more with specific subjects like reading, math, or writing, or was your trouble more about not paying attention in all subjects?\n\nPatient: I don't think it was a specific subject. It was more... general, I guess. I mean, even back in elementary school, my teachers would write that I was bright but careless. And... my mom usually had to sit with me to make sure I finished my homework. I would just lose track of assignments or forget to bring my books home. So... it wasn't the material, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========

{
  "candidates": ["F98.8"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": true
}
